The [ethics of war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics_of_war) investigates moral problems of war and violent conflicts. According to [just war theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Just_war_theory), waging war is morally justified if it fulfills certain conditions. These conditions are commonly divided into requirements concerning the [cause to initiate violent activities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jus_ad_bellum), such as self-defense, and the [way those violent activities are conducted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jus_in_bello), such as [avoiding excessive harm to civilians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proportionality_(law)#International_humanitarian_law) in the pursuit of [legitimate military targets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legitimate_military_target). [\[167\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-178) Military ethics is a closely related field that is interested in the conduct of [military personnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_personnel). It governs questions of the circumstances under which they are permitted to kill enemies, destroy infrastructure, and put the lives of their own troops at risk. [\[168\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-179) Additional topics are the recruitment, training, and discharge of military personnel. [\[169\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-180)
Other fields of applied ethics include [political ethics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_ethics), which examines the moral dimensions of political decisions, [\[170\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-181) educational ethics, which covers ethical issues related to proper teaching practices, [\[171\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-182) and [sexual ethics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_ethics), which addresses the moral implications of [sexual behavior](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_sexual_activity). [\[172\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-183)

#### Related fields

[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Ethics&action=edit&section=23)]

##### Value theory

[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Ethics&action=edit&section=24)]
Main article: [Value theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Value_theory)
Value theory, also called axiology, [\[l\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-185) is the philosophical study of value. It examines the nature and types of value. [\[174\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-186) A central distinction is between [intrinsic and instrumental value](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instrumental_and_intrinsic_value). An entity has intrinsic value if it is good in itself or good for its own sake. An entity has instrumental value if it is valuable as a means to something else, for example, by causing something that has intrinsic value. [\[175\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-187) Other topics include what kinds of things have value and how valuable they are. For instance, axiological hedonists say that [pleasure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pleasure) is the only source of intrinsic value and that the magnitude of value corresponds to the degree of pleasure. Axiological pluralists, by contrast, hold that there are different sources of intrinsic value, such as happiness, knowledge, and beauty. [\[176\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-188)
There are disagreements about the exact relation between value theory and ethics. Some philosophers characterize value theory as a subdiscipline of ethics while others see value theory as the broader term that encompasses other fields besides ethics, such as [aesthetics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aesthetics) and [political philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_philosophy). [\[177\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-189) A different characterization sees the two disciplines as overlapping but distinct fields. [\[178\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-190) The term [axiological ethics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axiological_ethics) is sometimes used for the discipline studying this overlap, that is, the part of ethics that studies values. [\[179\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-191) The two disciplines are sometimes distinguished based on their focus: ethics is about moral behavior or what is right while value theory is about value or what is [good](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Good). [\[180\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-192) Some ethical theories, like consequentialism, stand very close to value theory by defining what is right in terms of what is good. But this is not true for ethics in general and deontological theories tend to reject the idea that what is good can be used to define what is right. [\[181\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-193) [\[m\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-195)

##### Moral psychology

[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Ethics&action=edit&section=25)]
Main article: [Moral psychology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moral_psychology)
Moral psychology explores the psychological foundations and processes involved in moral behavior. It is an [empirical science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empirical_science) that studies how humans think and act in moral contexts. It is interested in how [moral reasoning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moral_reasoning) and judgments take place, how [moral character](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moral_character) forms, what sensitivity people have to moral evaluations, and how people attribute and react to [moral responsibility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moral_responsibility). [\[183\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-196)
One of its key topics is [moral development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moral_development) or the question of how morality develops on a psychological level from infancy to adulthood. [\[184\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-197) According to [Lawrence Kohlberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lawrence_Kohlberg), children go through different [stages of moral development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lawrence_Kohlberg%27s_stages_of_moral_development) as they understand moral principles first as fixed rules governing reward and punishment, then as conventional social norms, and later as abstract principles of what is objectively right across societies. [\[185\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-198) A closely related question is whether and how people can be [taught to act morally](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moral_education). [\[186\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-199)
[Evolutionary ethics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_ethics), a closely related field, explores how [evolutionary processes have shaped ethics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolution_of_morality). One of its key ideas is that [natural selection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_selection) is responsible for moral behavior and moral sensitivity. It interprets morality as an [adaptation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adaptation) to [evolutionary pressure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_pressure) that augments [fitness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fitness_(biology)) by offering a selective advantage. [\[187\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-200) [Altruism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Altruism), for example, can provide benefits to group survival by improving cooperation. [\[188\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-201) Some theorists, like [Mark Rowlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Rowlands), argue that morality is not limited to humans, meaning that some non-human animals act based on [moral emotions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moral_emotions). Others explore evolutionary precursors to morality in non-human animals. [\[189\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-202)

##### Descriptive ethics

[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Ethics&action=edit&section=26)]
Main article: [Descriptive ethics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Descriptive_ethics)
Descriptive ethics, also called comparative ethics, [\[190\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-203) studies existing moral codes, practices, and beliefs. It investigates and compares moral phenomena in different [societies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Societies) and different groups within a society. It aims to provide a [value-neutral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Value-neutral) and empirical description without judging or justifying which practices are objectively right. For instance, the question of how nurses think about the ethical implications of abortion belongs to descriptive ethics. Another example is descriptive business ethics, which describes ethical standards in the context of business, including common practices, official policies, and employee opinions. Descriptive ethics also has a historical dimension by exploring how moral practices and beliefs have changed over time. [\[191\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-204)
Descriptive ethics is a multidisciplinary field that is covered by disciplines such as [anthropology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthropology), [sociology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sociology), [psychology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychology), and [history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History). Its empirical outlook contrasts with the philosophical inquiry into normative questions, such as which ethical principles are correct and how to justify them. [\[192\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-205)

#### History

[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Ethics&action=edit&section=27)]
Main article: [History of ethics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_ethics)
According to [Laozi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laozi)'s teachings, which are central to conceptions of ethics in [Daoism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daoism), humans should aim to live in harmony with the [natural order of the universe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dao).
The history of ethics studies how moral philosophy has developed and evolved in the course of history. [\[193\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-206) It has its origin in ancient civilizations. In [ancient Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Egypt), the concept of [Maat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maat) was used as an ethical principle to guide behavior and maintain order by emphasizing the importance of truth, balance, and harmony. [\[194\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-207) [\[n\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-209) In [ancient India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_India) starting in the 2nd millennium BCE, [\[196\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-210) the [Vedas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vedas) and later [Upanishads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upanishads) were composed as the foundational texts of [Hindu philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu_philosophy) and discussed the role of [duty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dharma) and the [consequences of one's actions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karma). [\[197\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-211) [Buddhist ethics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_ethics) originated in ancient India between the 6th and 5th centuries BCE and advocated [compassion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karu%E1%B9%87%C4%81), [non-violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahimsa), and the pursuit of [enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enlightenment_in_Buddhism). [\[198\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-212) [Ancient China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_China) in the 6th century BCE [\[o\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-214) saw the emergence of [Confucianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confucianism), which focuses on moral conduct and [self-cultivation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-cultivation) by acting in agreement with virtues, and [Daoism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daoism), which teaches that human behavior should be in harmony with the [natural order of the universe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dao). [\[200\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-215)
In [ancient Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greece), [Socrates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socrates) ( c. 469–399 BCE) [\[201\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-216) emphasized the importance of inquiry into what a good life is by critically questioning established ideas and exploring concepts like virtue, justice, courage, and wisdom. [\[202\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-217) According to [Plato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plato) ( c. 428–347 BCE), [\[203\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-218) to lead a good life means that the different parts of the soul are in harmony with each other. [\[204\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-219) For [Aristotle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotle) (384–322 BCE), [\[205\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-220) a good life is associated with being happy by cultivating virtues and flourishing. [\[206\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-221) Starting in the 4th century BCE, the close relation between right action and happiness was also explored by the [Hellenistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenistic) schools of [Epicureanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epicureanism), which recommended a simple lifestyle without indulging in sensory pleasures, and [Stoicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stoicism), which advocated living in tune with reason and virtue while practicing self-mastery and becoming immune to disturbing emotions. [\[207\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-222)
Ethical thought in the [medieval period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval_period) was strongly influenced by religious teachings. [Christian philosophers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_philosophy) interpreted moral principles as [divine commands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divine_command) originating from God. [\[208\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-223) [Thomas Aquinas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Aquinas) (1224–1274 CE) [\[209\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-224) developed [natural law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_law) ethics by claiming that ethical behavior consists in following the laws and order of nature, which he believed were created by God. [\[210\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-225) In the Islamic world, philosophers like [Al-Farabi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Farabi) ( c. 878–950 CE) [\[211\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-226) and [Avicenna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avicenna) (980–1037 CE) [\[212\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-227) synthesized ancient Greek philosophy with the ethical teachings of Islam while emphasizing the harmony between reason and faith. [\[213\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-228) In medieval India, Hindu philosophers like [Adi Shankara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adi_Shankara) ( c. 700–750 CE) [\[214\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-229) and [Ramanuja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramanuja) (1017–1137 CE) [\[215\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-230) [\[p\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-232) saw the practice of spirituality to attain [liberation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moksha) as the highest goal of human behavior. [\[217\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-233)
[G. E. Moore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G._E._Moore)'s book *Principia Ethica* was partly responsible for the emergence of metaethics in the 20th century.
Moral philosophy in the modern period was characterized by a shift toward a secular approach to ethics. [Thomas Hobbes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Hobbes) (1588–1679) [\[218\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-234) identified self-interest as the primary drive of humans. He concluded that it would lead to "a war of every man against every man" unless a [social contract](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_contract) is established to avoid this outcome. [\[219\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-235) [David Hume](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Hume) (1711–1776) [\[220\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-236) thought that only moral sentiments, like [empathy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empathy), can motivate ethical actions while he saw reason not as a motivating factor but only as what anticipates the consequences of possible actions. [\[221\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-237) [Immanuel Kant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immanuel_Kant) (1724–1804), [\[222\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-238) by contrast, saw reason as the source of morality. He formulated a [deontological theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deontology), according to which the ethical value of actions depends on their conformity with moral laws independent of their outcome. These laws take the form of [categorical imperatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Categorical_imperative), which are universal requirements that apply to every situation. [\[223\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-239)
[Georg Wilhelm Friedrich Hegel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georg_Wilhelm_Friedrich_Hegel) (1770–1831) [\[224\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-240) saw Kant's categorical imperative on its own as an empty formalism and emphasized the role of social institutions in providing concrete content to moral duties. [\[225\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-241) According to the Christian philosophy of [Søren Kierkegaard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%B8ren_Kierkegaard) (1813–1855), [\[226\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-242) the [demands of ethical duties are sometimes suspended](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fear_and_Trembling#Teleological_suspension_of_the_ethical) when doing God's will. [\[227\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-243) [Friedrich Nietzsche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friedrich_Nietzsche) (1844–1900) [\[228\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-244) formulated criticisms of both Christian and Kantian morality. [\[229\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-245) Another influential development in this period was the formulation of [utilitarianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Utilitarianism) by [Jeremy Bentham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeremy_Bentham) (1748–1832) [\[230\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-246) and [John Stuart Mill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Stuart_Mill) (1806–1873). [\[231\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-247) According to the utilitarian doctrine, actions should promote happiness while reducing suffering and the right action is the one that produces the greatest good for the greatest number of people. [\[232\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-248)
[Simone de Beauvoir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simone_de_Beauvoir) explored moral philosophy from the perspective of [existentialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Existentialism). [\[233\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-249)
An important development in [20th-century ethics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contemporary_ethics) in [analytic philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Analytic_philosophy) was the emergence of metaethics. [\[234\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-250) Significant early contributions to this field were made by [G. E. Moore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G._E._Moore) (1873–1958), [\[235\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-251) who argued that moral values are essentially different from other [properties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Property_(philosophy)) found in the natural world. [\[236\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-252) [R. M. Hare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R._M._Hare) (1919–2002) [\[237\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-253) followed this idea in formulating his [prescriptivism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_prescriptivism), which states that moral statements are commands that, unlike regular [judgments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judgment), are neither true nor false. [\[238\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-254) [J. L. Mackie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._L._Mackie) (1917–1981) [\[239\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-255) suggested that every moral statement is false since there are no moral facts. [\[123\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-books.google.com-134) An influential argument for moral realism was made by [Derek Parfit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Derek_Parfit) (1942–2017), [\[240\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-256) who argued that morality concerns objective features of reality that give people reasons to act in one way or another. [\[241\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-257) [Bernard Williams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernard_Williams) (1929–2003) [\[242\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-258) agreed with the close relation between reasons and ethics but defended a subjective view instead that sees reasons as internal [mental states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mental_states) that may or may not reflect external reality. [\[243\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-259)
Another development in this period was the revival of ancient [virtue ethics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virtue_ethics) by philosophers like [Philippa Foot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippa_Foot) (1920–2010). [\[244\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-260) In the field of political philosophy, [John Rawls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Rawls) (1921–2002) [\[245\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-261) relied on [Kantian ethics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kantian_ethics) to analyze [social justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_justice) as [a form of fairness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justice_as_Fairness). [\[246\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-262) In continental philosophy, [phenomenologists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phenomenology_(philosophy)) such as [Max Scheler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Max_Scheler) (1874–1928) [\[247\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-263) and [Nicolai Hartmann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicolai_Hartmann) (1882–1950) [\[248\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-264) built ethical systems based on the claim that values have objective reality that can be investigated using the phenomenological method. [\[249\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-265) Existentialists like [Jean-Paul Sartre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Paul_Sartre) (1905–1980) [\[250\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-266) and [Simone de Beauvoir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simone_de_Beauvoir) (1908–1986), [\[251\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-267) by contrast, held that values are created by humans and explored the consequences of this view in relation to individual freedom, responsibility, and authenticity. [\[252\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-268) This period also saw the emergence of [feminist ethics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feminist_ethics), which questions traditional ethical assumptions associated with a male perspective and puts alternative concepts, like [care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics_of_care), at the center. [\[253\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-269)

##### Notes

[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Ethics&action=edit&section=30)]
1. **^** According to some theorists, *teleological ethics* is a wider term than *consequentialism* because it also covers certain forms of virtue ethics. [\[19\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-19)
1. **^** This state is known as [supererogation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supererogation). [\[37\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-38)
1. **^** A maxim is a rule that people can adopt to guide their action, like "If you want to make big money, you should go into sales" or "Thou shalt not commit murder". [\[54\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-56)
1. **^** Some ethicists state that contractualism is not a normative ethical theory but a metaethical theory because of its emphasis on how moral norms are justified. [\[61\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-64)
1. **^** The ancient Greeks used the word *arête* , which has the meaning of both 'virtue' and 'excellence'. [\[69\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-73)
1. **^** Ontology is the branch of philosophy that studies the nature and categories of being. [\[89\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-94)
1. **^** This contrast is closely related to the [is–ought problem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Is%E2%80%93ought_problem) problem, first articulated by [David Hume](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Hume), which states that one cannot deduce a normative statement from a descriptive statement. [\[96\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-102) The exact relation between these types of statements is disputed. [\[97\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-103)
1. **^** [Deontic logic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deontic_logic) provides a [formal system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formal_system) describing the logical relations between these and similar concepts. [\[100\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-auto-107)
1. **^** Some philosophers suggest that there is [moral luck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moral_luck), which occurs when factors outside a person's control influence the moral status of that person. [\[105\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-113)
1. **^** This position can be understood by analogy to [Einstein's theory of relativity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Einstein%27s_theory_of_relativity), which states that the magnitude of physical properties like mass, length, and duration depends on the [frame of reference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frame_of_reference) of the observer. [\[112\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-121)
1. **^** For example, [natural law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_law) ethics, an influential position in [Christian ethics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_ethics), says that morality is based on a natural law created by God. [\[118\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-128)
1. **^** There are disagreements in the academic literature about whether they are synonyms or whether one or the other is the wider term. [\[173\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-184)
1. **^** For example, the deontologist [David Ross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/W._D._Ross) holds that the principles of right actions are distinct from the principles of value. He says that the terms *good* and *right* mean different things and should not be confused with one another. [\[182\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-194)
1. **^** The first explicit discussions of Maat as a concept date to about 2100 BCE. [\[195\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-208)
1. **^** Dates for the emergence of Daoism are disputed and some theorists suggest a later date between the 4th and 3rd centuries BCE. [\[199\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-213)
1. **^** Modern scholars have questioned these traditionally cited dates, suggesting that Ramanuja's life ran from 1077 to 1157. [\[216\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics#cite_note-231)
